\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{p}^{\vee},\mathfrak{q}^{\vee}),\,\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p})
is well-studied, see for example
[
BLPW16
, Section 10.2.2]
(note that in this case varieties above are type
A
quiver varieties, see
[
Maf05
]
). On the other hand, not much is known outside of type
A
. For example, we do not know how to describe the space
\mathfrak{h}_{X^{\vee}}=H^{2}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{p}^{\vee},\mathfrak{q}^{\vee}),\mathbb{C})
in general. Namely, recall that for
\mathfrak{p}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}
, we have
\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{b}^{\vee},\mathfrak{q}^{\vee})=T^{*}(G^{\vee}/Q^{\vee})
, so
H^{2}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{p}^{\vee},\mathfrak{q}^{\vee}),\mathbb{C})=\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{m}^{\vee})
. For
\mathfrak{q}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{b}^{\vee}
, in most cases we have
\mathfrak{h}_{X^{\vee}}=\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}
by Proposition
5.2
(ii) so in general one might guess that in some cases the natural restriction homomorphism
H^{2}(T^{*}(G^{\vee}/Q^{\vee}),\mathbb{C})\rightarrow H^{2}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{p}^{\vee},\mathfrak{q}^{\vee}),\mathbb{C})
is surjective. A case by case analysis similar to
[
LNS12
]
can be done, but we do not know any general result on when this is the case.

For example, if
\mathfrak{m}^{\vee}\simeq\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{p}(2)\times\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}(1)\times\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}(1)\subset\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{p}(6)
and
\mathfrak{l}^{\vee}\simeq\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}(3)
, the map
H^{2}(T^{*}(G^{\vee}/Q^{\vee}),\mathbb{C})\rightarrow H^{2}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{p}^{\vee},\mathfrak{q}^{\vee}),\mathbb{C})
is surjective, see
Appendix

A
. However, if we consider
\mathfrak{m}^{\vee}\simeq\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}(7)\times\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}(1)\times\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{o}(5)\subset\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{o}(21)
and
\mathfrak{l}^{\vee}\simeq\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}(3)^{\times 3}\times\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{o}(3)\subset\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{o}(21)
, it is easy to show that the map
H^{2}(T^{*}(G^{\vee}/Q^{\vee}),\mathbb{C})\rightarrow H^{2}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{p}^{\vee},\mathfrak{q}^{\vee}),\mathbb{C})
cannot be surjective by computing dimensions of both sides.

Finally, we note that the description of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{z}_{X}
seems to be even more complicated. We are not aware of any general results in this direction.

The goal of this paper is to investigate the Hikita-Nakajima conjecture for the pair
\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{p}^{\vee}_{-},\mathfrak{q}^{\vee}_{-})
,
\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p})
. We will see that it is
not
correct as stated (outside of type
A
) but has a natural modification that we will prove. In type
A
, our modification will imply the actual Hikita-Nakajima conjecture (see Appendix
D
).

In general, it would be very interesting to study the symplectic duality for the pair
\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{p}^{\vee}_{-},\mathfrak{q}^{\vee}_{-})
,
\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p})
. Note that although many things are not known, these varieties are very explicit and closely related to the “classical” Lie theory. For example, by
[
Web11
]
, categories
\mathcal{O}
over the quantizations of
\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p})
are equivalent to
parabolic singular
categories
\mathcal{O}
. One of the predictions of symplectic duality is that the categories
\mathcal{O}
for dual varieties are
Koszul dual
(see
[
BLPW16
, Section 10.1]
). For the pair as above, this statement indeed follows from
[
Bac99
]
and
[
Web11
]
.

Let us also emphasize that there is an interesting combinatorics associated with the varieties
\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p})
. See, for example, the description of the torus fixed points of
\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p})
given in Appendix
E
. In type
A
, it gives an alternative (more “Lie theoretic”) way of thinking about the fixed points of the type
A
Nakajima quiver varieties (compare with
[
Din22
, Section 3.4]
). The explicit relation between two descriptions of fixed points is a part of the work in progress
[
KL
]
. We discuss the expected combinatorial trace of the Hikita-Nakajima conjecture in
Section

10.3
.

6.
Hikita conjecture for nilpotent orbits: additional structures and counterexamples

In this section, we recall additional structures that we have on both sides of the classical Hikita-Nakajima conjecture for
X^{\vee}=S(\chi^{\vee})\cap\mathcal{N}^{\vee}
and
X=\Spec(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])
. While they share many similarities, we observe the differences that will subsequently provide counterexamples to the original Hikita conjecture and lead to its modification that we propose in
Section

8
. To simplify the notations, we denote by
H^{*}_{?}(\bullet)
,
H_{*}^{?}(\bullet)
the equivariant cohomology and Borel-Moore homology with coefficients in
\mathbb{C}
respectively.

6.1.
Cohomological side

Recall that
T_{X^{\vee}}=T_{\chi^{\vee}}\simeq T_{e^{\vee}}\subset T^{\vee}
is a maximal torus in
Z_{e^{\vee}}
, the pointwise centralizer of
\{e^{\vee},h^{\vee},f^{\vee}\}
in
G^{\vee}
. Torus
T_{\chi^{\vee}}
acts naturally on
\widetilde{S}(\chi^{\vee})
and
\mathcal{B}_{\chi^{\vee}}
. Write
L^{\vee}
for the connected component of the centralizer of
T_{\chi^{\vee}}
in
G^{\vee}
. Let
\mathfrak{h}_{\chi^{\vee}}=\operatorname{Lie}T_{\chi^{\vee}}
. At the cohomological side of the Hikita-Nakajima conjecture we deal with the
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{\chi^{\vee}}]
-algebra
H^{*}_{T_{\chi^{\vee}}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi^{\vee}))
. The
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{\chi^{\vee}}]
-module
H^{*}_{T_{e^{\vee}}}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee}))
has the natural structure of a module over the Weyl group
W
of
G^{\vee}
(via the standard “Springer” action). Moreover, this action of
W
can be extended to an action of
\widetilde{W}=S^{\bullet}\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}\#W
as follows.

Consider the identification
H^{2}_{T_{\chi^{\vee}}}(T^{*}\mathcal{B}^{\vee},\mathbb{C})\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{\chi^{\vee}}
, then the element
\xi\in\mathfrak{h}
acts on
H^{*}_{T_{\chi^{\vee}}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi^{\vee}))
via the multiplication by the image of
\xi\in H^{2}_{T_{\chi^{\vee}}}(T^{*}\mathcal{B}^{\vee},\mathbb{C})
under the restriction homomorphism
H^{2}_{T_{\chi^{\vee}}}(T^{*}\mathcal{B}^{\vee})\rightarrow H^{2}_{T_{\chi^{\vee}}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi^{\vee}))
.

Remark 6.1
.

More generally, one can consider the convolution algebra
H^{G^{\vee}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}_{*}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{\vee}\times_{\mathcal{N}^{\vee}}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{\vee})
that is isomorphic to the
degenerate affine Hecke algebra

H
. Recall that
H
is the graded algebra over
\mathbb{C}[\hbar]
, that specializes to
\widetilde{W}
for
\hbar=0
(in particular, we have the identification
H^{G^{\vee}}_{*}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{\vee}\times_{\mathcal{N}^{\vee}}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{\vee})\simeq\widetilde{W}
). The convolution algebra
H^{G^{\vee}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}_{*}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{\vee}\times_{\mathcal{N}^{\vee}}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{\vee})
acts naturally on

H_{*}^{T_{\chi^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi^{\vee}))\simeq H^{*}_{T_{\chi^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi^{\vee}))=H^{*}_{T_{\chi^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\mathcal{B}_{\chi^{\vee}}),

where the first isomorphism is given by the Poincare duality.Similarly, algebra
H
acts naturally on
H_{*}^{T_{\chi^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\mathcal{B}_{\chi^{\vee}})
, and modules
H^{*}_{T_{\chi^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\mathcal{B}_{\chi^{\vee}})
,
H_{*}^{T_{\chi^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\mathcal{B}_{\chi^{\vee}})
are dual to each other.

Let us now describe the irreducible modules over
\widetilde{W}
geometrically (more generally, the analogous description of irreducible
H
-modules is given in
[
Lus02
, Theorem 1.15]
).

Irreducible
\widetilde{W}
-modules are parametrized by conjugacy classes of triples
(\chi^{\vee},x,\rho)
, where
\chi^{\vee}\in\mathcal{N}^{\vee}
is a nilpotent element,
x\in\mathfrak{z}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}}(\chi^{\vee})
is semisimple, and
\rho
is an irreducible representation of
A(e^{\vee},x):=Z_{G^{\vee}}(e^{\vee},x)/Z_{G^{\vee}}(e^{\vee},x)^{0}
that appears in
H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{\chi^{\vee}}^{x})
. Namely, we can assume that
x\in\mathfrak{h}_{\chi^{\vee}}
and consider the specialization
H^{*}_{T_{\chi^{\vee}}}(\widetilde{S}_{\chi^{\vee}})_{x}=H^{*}(\widetilde{S}_{\chi^{\vee}}^{x})
(see Section
3.1.1
). Let
\mathfrak{l}_{x}^{\vee}=\mathfrak{z}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}}(x)
. We note that if
x
is generic then
\mathfrak{l}_{x}^{\vee}=Z_{\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}}(T_{\chi^{\vee}})=\mathfrak{l}^{\vee}
.

It is easy to see (compare with
[
Kat83
, Proposition 6.2]
), that we have isomorphisms of
A(e^{\vee},x)\times\widetilde{W}
-modules (induced by the natural pushforward maps):

\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{W}_{L_{x}}}^{\widetilde{W}}H_{*}(\mathcal{B}_{L_{x}^{\vee},e^{\vee}})\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,H_{*}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}}^{x}),~\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{W}_{L_{x}}}^{\widetilde{W}}H_{*}(\widetilde{S}_{L^{\vee}_{x}}(e^{\vee}))\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,H_{*}(\widetilde{S}({e^{\vee}})^{x}).

It induces the isomorphism of
\widetilde{W}
-modules:

(6.1.1)

\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{W}_{L_{x}}}^{\widetilde{W}}H_{*}(\mathcal{B}_{L_{x}^{\vee},e^{\vee}})_{\rho}\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,H_{*}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}}^{x})_{\rho},~\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{W}_{L_{x}}}^{\widetilde{W}}H_{*}(\widetilde{S}_{L^{\vee}_{x}}(e^{\vee}))_{\rho}\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,H_{*}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee})^{x})_{\rho},

where
\bullet_{\rho}
is the multiplicity space of
\rho
.

Module
H_{*}(\widetilde{S}^{x}(e^{\vee}))_{\rho}
is called the
costandard
module over
\widetilde{W}
corresponding to
(e^{\vee},x,\rho)
. The dual module
H_{*}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}}^{x})_{\rho}
is called the
standard
module. The image of the natural morphism
H_{*}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}}^{x})_{\rho}\rightarrow H_{*}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee})^{x})_{\rho}
is the unique irreducible quotient of
H_{*}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}}^{x})_{\rho}
and is the unique irreducible submodule of
H_{*}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee})^{x})_{\rho}
(compare with
[
CG10
, Theorem 8.1.13]
).
Let us describe this irreducible module explicitly. It follows from (
6.1.1
) that the module we are dealing with is nothing else but

\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{W}_{L_{x}}}^{\widetilde{W}}(\operatorname{Im}(H_{*}(\mathcal{B}_{L_{x}^{\vee},e^{\vee}})_{\rho}\rightarrow H_{*}(\widetilde{S}_{L_{x}^{\vee}}(e^{\vee}))_{\rho}))=\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{W}_{L_{x}}}^{\widetilde{W}}H^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathcal{B}_{L_{x}^{\vee},e^{\vee}})_{\rho}.

To see that, recall that
H_{*}(\mathcal{B}_{L_{x}^{\vee},e^{\vee}})
lives in degrees
0,1,\ldots,2d
, where
d=\operatorname{dim}\mathcal{B}_{L_{x}^{\vee},e^{\vee}}
while
H_{*}(\widetilde{S}_{L_{x}^{\vee}}(e^{\vee}))\simeq H^{4d-*}(\widetilde{S}_{L_{x}^{\vee}}(e^{\vee}))\simeq H^{4d-*}(\mathcal{B}_{L_{x}^{\vee},e^{\vee}})
lives in degrees
2d,2d+1,\ldots,4d
so we see that the only term that survives is the degree
2d
term i.e. the image of
H_{2d}(\mathcal{B}_{L_{x}^{\vee},e^{\vee}})\rightarrow H_{2d}(\widetilde{S}_{L_{x}^{\vee}}(e^{\vee}))\simeq H^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathcal{B}_{L_{x}^{\vee},e^{\vee}})
. It remains to note that the latter map is an isomorphism.

Remark 6.2
.

Note that the module
\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{W}_{L_{x}}}^{\widetilde{W}}H^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathcal{B}_{L_{x}^{\vee},e^{\vee}})_{\rho}
is indeed irreducible: it follows from the Springer theory that
H^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathcal{B}_{L_{x}^{\vee},e^{\vee}})_{\rho}
is an irreducible
W_{L_{x}}
-module. Note also that the action of
\mathfrak{h}
on
H^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathcal{B}_{L_{x}^{\vee},e^{\vee}})_{\rho}
is via
x
(i.e., an element
\xi\in\mathfrak{h}
acts via the multiplication by
\xi(x)
, here we identify
\mathfrak{h}
with
(\mathfrak{h}^{\vee})^{*}
and use the embedding
\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}\subset\mathfrak{h}^{\vee}
). Note now that the stabilizer of
x
in
W
is precisely
W_{L_{x}}
, so we conclude from the Mackey theory that
\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{W}_{L_{x}}}^{\widetilde{W}}H^{\mathrm{top}}(\mathcal{B}_{L_{x}^{\vee},e^{\vee}})_{\rho}
is irreducible.

So, the cohomological side
H^{*}_{T_{e^{\vee}}}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee}))
of the classical equivariant Hikita-Nakajima conjecture is a
\widetilde{W}
-module over
S^{\bullet}\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}
that is free over
S^{\bullet}\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}
. Moreover, its specialization at every
x\in\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}
decomposes as the direct sum
H^{*}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee})^{x})=\bigoplus_{\rho\in\operatorname{Irrep}(A(e^{\vee},x))}\rho\otimes H^{*}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee})^{x})_{\rho}
.
Taking
x=0
, we see that that if the action of
A(e^{\vee})
on
H^{*}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee}))
is
nontrivial
then module
H^{*}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee}))
is
not
cyclic.

Assume now that
e^{\vee}
is
regular
in
\mathfrak{l}^{\vee}
, the Lie algebra of
L^{\vee}
. Let
\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}^{\mathrm{reg}}\subset\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}
be the set of
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}=\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{l})^{*}
such that
Z_{\mathfrak{g}}(\lambda)=\mathfrak{l}
, i.e.,
\langle\lambda,\beta^{\vee}\rangle\neq 0
for any
\beta\in\Delta_{\mathfrak{p}}\setminus\Delta_{\mathfrak{l}}
. The module
H^{*}_{T_{e^{\vee}}}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee}))|_{x}=H^{*}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee})^{x})=H^{\mathrm{top}}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee})^{x})
is irreducible for
x\in\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}^{\mathrm{reg}}
and is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{W}_{L}}^{\widetilde{W}}\mathbb{C}_{x}
. Moreover, it is cyclic over
S^{\bullet}\mathfrak{h}
and is generated by
[1]\in H^{*}_{T_{e^{\vee}}}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee}))|_{x}
in this case. In other words, we have an isomorphism of
\widetilde{W}
-modules over
\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}^{\mathrm{reg}}
:

H^{*}_{T_{e^{\vee}}}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee}))|_{\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}^{\mathrm{reg}}}\simeq\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{W}_{L}}^{\widetilde{W}}\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}^{\mathrm{reg}}],

and
H^{*}_{T_{e^{\vee}}}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee}))|_{\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}^{\mathrm{reg}}}
is generated by
1
over
S^{\bullet}\mathfrak{h}\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}^{\mathrm{reg}}]
.

6.2.
Fixed points side

Let
\widetilde{X}
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminalization of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])
. The action of the torus
T
on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])
extends to an action on the deformation
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])_{\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}}
over
\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}
. Moreover, the map
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])_{\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}
is
T
-equivariant. Note that the action of
T
on
\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}
is identified with the action of
T
on
H^{2}(\widetilde{X}^{reg},\mathbb{C})
that is trivial. Thus, we have a natural homomorphism

(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])_{\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}})^{T}\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}.

The pullback gives an action of the algebra
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]
on
\mathbb{C}[(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])_{\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}})^{T}]
. It naturally extends to an action of the algebra
\widetilde{W}=S^{\bullet}\mathfrak{h}\#W
, the action of
W
is induced by the adjoint action of
N(T)\subset G
.

Module
\mathbb{C}[(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])_{\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}})^{T}]
may contain a torsion considered as a module over
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}]
(in particular, it may not be flat over
\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}
in general), see Example
6.7
below. To elaborate on that we want to explicitly state the ”flatness conditions”.

Flatness condition
.

The algebra
\mathbb{C}[X_{\mathfrak{h}_{X}}^{T}]
is flat over
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{X}]
.

Assume that
X
has a unique
T
-fixed point, and therefore the algebra
\mathbb{C}[X_{\mathfrak{h}_{X}}^{T}]
is finitely generated over
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{X}]
. Then for any point
\eta\in\mathfrak{h}_{X}
the algebra
\mathbb{C}[X_{\eta}^{T}]
is finite-dimensional. We can state a weaker version of the condition above that is easier to verify.

Weak flatness condition
.

Let
\eta_{0}\in\mathfrak{h}_{X}
be a generic point. For any point
\eta\in\mathfrak{h}_{X}
, we have

(
\spadesuit
)

\dim\mathbb{C}[X_{\eta}^{T}]=\dim\mathbb{C}[X_{\eta_{0}}^{T}].

We remark that if
X
has a unique fixed point, the Flatness condition automatically implies the Weak flatness condition. In particular, this always happens when
X=\Spec({\mathbb{C}}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{\vee}])
. The unique fixed
T
-point in
\overline{\mathbb{O}}^{\vee}
is the point
0
, and therefore
X
has finitely many fixed points. Arguing as in
Lemma

2.44
, it implies that
X
has a unique fixed point. And thus
{\mathbb{C}}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{\vee}]
is a local algebra.

Moreover, when
\widetilde{D}({\mathbb{O}}^{\vee})
is an actual nilpotent orbit (not a cover) and its closure in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is normal,
{\mathbb{C}}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{\vee}]
is generated by
1
as a
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{g}^{*}]
-module, and thus
\mathbb{C}[\Spec({\mathbb{C}}[\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}^{\vee}])^{T}]
is generated by
1
as a
S^{\bullet}\mathfrak{h}
-module. Note also that
\mathbb{C}[(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee}))])^{T}]
is indecomposable already over
S^{\bullet}\mathfrak{h}
in this case (any local ring is indecomposable as a module over itself).

Assume now that
e^{\vee}
is regular in some Levi
\mathfrak{l}^{\vee}
. As we discussed,
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])_{\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}}
is nothing else but
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[T^{*}_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})}\mathcal{P}]
in this case (recall that we have the natural identification
\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})=\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}
). Moreover, the morphism
T^{*}_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})}\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[T^{*}_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})}\mathcal{P}]
of schemes over
\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})
becomes an isomorphism over
\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})^{\mathrm{reg}}=\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}^{\mathrm{reg}}
(recal that
\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}^{\mathrm{reg}}
consists of
\lambda
s.t.
Z_{\mathfrak{g}}(\lambda)=\mathfrak{l}
).
In other words, the restriction
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])_{\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}^{\mathrm{reg}}}
identifies with
G\times^{L}\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}^{\mathrm{reg}}
, see
Lemma

2.12
, and is a closed subvariety of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\times_{\mathfrak{h}^{*}/W}\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}^{\mathrm{reg}}
via the map
[(g,x)]\mapsto gx
. It is then easy to see that after passing to
T
-fixed points, the corresponding
\widetilde{W}
-module is nothing else but
\operatorname{Ind}_{\widetilde{W}_{L}}^{\widetilde{W}}\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})^{\mathrm{reg}}]
(in this case this is a simple computation, but it will also follow from the results of Section
9
).

6.3.
Cohomological vs fixed points side

In the Hikita-Nakajima conjecture we are comparing two algebras over
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}]
:
\mathbb{C}[(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])_{\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}})^{T}]
and
H^{*}_{T_{e^{\vee}}}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee}))
. As we saw in the previous sections, both of them are
\widetilde{W}
-modules. It is natural to expect that the (conjectural) identification of these algebras should be compatible with the
\widetilde{W}
-module structures. However, from the discussion in previous sections it is clear that these modules are not isomorphic in general.

The differences are:

(1)

Module
H^{*}_{T_{e^{\vee}}}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee}))
is
always
free over
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}]
while
\mathbb{C}[(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])_{\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}})^{T}]
might have torsion (see Example
6.7
).

(2)

If
\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
is an
orbit
with
normal
closure then the module
\mathbb{C}[(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})]))^{T}]
is
cyclic
indecomposable over
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]
with generator
1
but
H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}})
is not indecomposable in general (see Example
6.6
).

So, we see that one should not expect, in general, that the algebras above will be isomorphic.
On the other hand, If
e^{\vee}
is regular in
\mathfrak{l}^{\vee}
, then the restrictions of
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}]
-modules
\mathbb{C}[(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])_{\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}})^{\mathbb{C}^{\times}}]
,
H^{*}_{T_{e^{\vee}}}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee}))
to
\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})^{\mathrm{reg}}
are
isomorphic
as
\widetilde{W}
-modules and are moreover generated by
1
over
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}^{\mathrm{reg}}]
. Thus, they are isomorphic as
algebras
and the
\widetilde{W}
-action on them is uniquely determined by the action of
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]
.
So, the natural guess for the modification of the original Hikita-Nakajima conjecture would be as follows.

Instead of considering the whole
\widetilde{W}
-modules as above, we can consider their
\widetilde{W}\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}]
-submodules generated by the unit elements
1
(note that this does not change restrictions of our modules to
\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}^{\mathrm{reg}}
but affects restrictions to other points of
\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}
). Since the action of
W
on
1
is trivial, this simply reduces to considering images:

\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}]\rightarrow H^{*}_{T_{e^{\vee}}}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee}))),~\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])_{\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}})^{T}).

Warning 6.3
.

Note that taking images does not commute with passing to fibers.

This resolves the issue (
2
) (both of the modules are now
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}]
-cyclic by the definition) but may not resolve the issue
(\ref{issue_1})
(namely, the second
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}]
-module may not be torsion free over
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}]
, see Example
6.7
). To resolve this, we use the natural pull back homomorphism
\mathbb{C}[\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])_{\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}}]^{T})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[(T^{*}_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})}\mathcal{P})^{T}]
and replace the second algebra by:

\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[(T^{*}_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})}\mathcal{P})^{T}].

Concluding, our modification of the Hikita-Nakajima conjecture in this case claims that there exists the isomorphism of
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}]
-algebras:

\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}]\rightarrow H^{*}_{T_{e^{\vee}}}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee})))\simeq\operatorname{Im}(\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[(T^{*}_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})}\mathcal{P})^{T}]

We note that if
\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})
is an orbit with normal closure and
\mathbb{C}[\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])_{\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}}^{T}]
is flat over
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}]
(for example
\mathfrak{g}
is of type A), then the algebra on the RHS is precisely
\mathbb{C}[\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])_{\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}}^{T}]
. In particular, if
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
, then our statement recovers the actual Hikita-Nakajima conjecture, so it gives an alternative transparent “Lie theoretic” proof of this statement (proved before by Alex Weekes in his PhD thesis, see also
[
KTWWY19
]
using realizations of the varieties above as slices in the affine Grassmannians). See Appendix
D
for details.

Remark 6.4
.

Note that we are not discussing the equivariant Hikita-Nakajima conjecture in this Section. Recall that the RHS of this conjecture is
H^{*}_{T_{e^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee}))
which is a module over the degenerate affine Hecke algebra
{\bf{H}}
(see
[
Lus88
, Section 0.1]
) that is a deformation of
\widetilde{W}
over
\mathbb{C}[\hbar]
. The LHS of the Hikita conjecture is
\mathsf{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{h}_{e^{\vee}}}(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})])))
. We
do not know
how to construct the action of
{\bf{H}}
on the latter.

6.4.
Counterexamples to the Hikita-Nakajima conjectures

The goal of this section is to give counterexamples to the original Hikita conjecture for a pair
\widetilde{S}(e^{\vee})
,
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{D}(\mathbb{O}^{\vee})]
supporting our observations in the previous section. We will see that counterexamples already exist in the “best possible setting”, namely, even if we put the following restrictions on
e^{\vee}
.

(1)

The element
e^{\vee}
is regular in the Lie algebra of some Levi subgroup
L^{\vee}\subset G^{\vee}
.

(2)

The refined BVLS duality
\widetilde{D}
sends the orbit
{\mathbb{O}}^{\vee}=G^{\vee}.e^{\vee}
to the orbit of a nilpotent element
e
(i.e,
e
is birationally induced from
0
orbit in
L
). Furthermore, we assume that the closure
\overline{G.e}
is normal.

In this setting, the classical Hikita conjecture predicts an isomorphism of algebra

(6.4.1)

\mathbb{C}[\overline{G.e}\cap\mathfrak{h}]\simeq H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}}).

Recall that we fixed the identification
\mathfrak{g}^{*}\simeq\mathfrak{g}
. Consider a generic element
x\in\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{l})
. Following
[
Kra80
, Proposition 4]
, we describe a quotient of the algebra
\mathbb{C}[\overline{G.e}\cap\mathfrak{h}]
using
x
.

Let
J^{\prime}
be the defining ideal of
G.x
in
\mathfrak{g}
. We have a natural grading of
J^{\prime}
coming from
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{g}]
. Then
J_{e}=\gr J^{\prime}
is the defining ideal of
\overline{G.e}
in
\mathfrak{g}
. Now let
I^{\prime}
be the ideal generated by
J^{\prime}
and the coordinate functions
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Delta}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
of the non-diagonal entries of
\mathfrak{g}
. And write
I_{e}
for the defining ideal of
\overline{G.e}\cap\mathfrak{h}
in the algebra
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]
. Then we have
I_{e}\subset\gr I^{\prime}
. Thus, we get a surjective
\widetilde{W}
-equivariant map of algebras
\mathbb{C}[\overline{G.e}\cap\mathfrak{h}]\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]/\gr I^{\prime}
.

We have the following proposition.

Proposition 6.5
.

In the setting above, the classical Hikita conjecture is equivalent to two statements.

(1)

The quotient morphism
\mathbb{C}[\overline{G.e}\cap\mathfrak{h}]\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]/\gr I^{\prime}
is an isomorphism.

(2)

We have an isomorphism of rings
H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}})\simeq\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]/\gr I^{\prime}
.

Proof.

If both conditions hold, then
\mathbb{C}[\overline{G.e}\cap\mathfrak{h}]\simeq\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]/\gr I^{\prime}\simeq H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}})
. On the other hand, assuming that the classical Hikita conjecture is true, we have
\mathbb{C}[\overline{G.e}\cap\mathfrak{h}]\simeq H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}})
. Then we must have
\dim\mathbb{C}[\overline{G.e}\cap\mathfrak{h}]=

\dim H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}})

=|W/W_{L^{\vee}}|
. This is the dimension of
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]/\operatorname{gr}I^{\prime}
. Therefore, it follows that the surjection in (1) of
Proposition

6.5
is an isomorphism.
∎

We remark that the proof implies that (1) is equivalent to the weak flatness condition

♠

‣
Weak flatness condition
.

Let us now give several examples in types B-C in which at least one of the statements in
Proposition

6.5
fails to hold. In other words, the classical Hikita conjecture does not apply in these cases.

In these examples, one can check that the elements
e
we choose are birationally Richardson by following
[
Fu03a
, Section 3]
. The normality of orbit closures follows from the table at the end of
[
KP82
]
.

The following example showcases a situation in which Statement 1 in
Proposition

6.5
is true, but Statement 2 is not.

Example 6.6
.

Let
G=Sp(6)
and
G^{\vee}=SO(7)
, they have the same Weyl group
W=S_{3}\rtimes(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus 3}
. Let
e^{\vee}\in G^{\vee}
be a nilpotent element with the associated partition
(3,3,1)
. The refined BVLS duality sends the orbit of
e^{\vee}
in
\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{o}(7)
to the orbit of
e
, a nilpotent element with the associated partition
(2,2,2)
in
\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{p}(6)
.

The function side
. In this case, we have
L^{\vee}
and
L
are isomorphic to
GL(3)
. We choose an embedding
\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{p}(6)\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}(6)
such that we can realize
\mathfrak{h}
as the set of diagonal matrices diag
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},-x_{3},-x_{2},-x_{1})
.

A generic element
x\in Z_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathfrak{l})
has the form diag
(t,t,t,-t,-t,-t)
for
t\neq 0
. Fix such a
t
, identify
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]
with
\mathbb{C}[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}]
. The defining ideal
I^{\prime}
of the orbit
W.x\subset\mathfrak{h}
is generated by the elements
(x_{i}^{2}-t^{2})_{i=1,2,3}
. As a consequence,
\gr I^{\prime}
is
(x_{i}^{2})_{i=1,2,3}
. Furthermore, we claim that
I_{e}=\gr I^{\prime}
in this case. Since the partition of
e
is
(2,2,2)
, any element
X
in the closure
\overline{G.e}
has the property
X^{2}=0
. So
x_{i}^{2}\in I_{e}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 3
, and
I_{e}=\gr I^{\prime}
; Statement 1 in
Proposition

6.5
is verified. The algebra
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]/I_{e}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}]/(x_{1}^{2},x_{2}^{2},x_{3}^{2})
.

The cohomological side
. We first compute the Betti numbers of
\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}}
using the algorithms provided in
[
Kim18
, Section 8]
. The partition of
e^{\vee}
is
(3,3,1)
, so the component group of
C_{G}(e)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. This group acts on each
H^{2i}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}})
with two irreducible characters, the trivial character and the sign character. Let
n_{i}^{+}
(resp.
n_{i}^{-}
) denote the multiplicity of the trivial (resp. sign) character in
H^{2i}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}})
. In
[
Kim18
, Proposition 8.2]
, the closed formulas of
n_{i}
for the partition
(2k+1,2k+1,1)
are worked out as follows

n_{i}^{+}={2k+1\choose i},n_{i}^{-}={2k+1\choose i-2}\quad\text{for}\quad 0\leqslant 2i\leqslant 2k+3.

Write
d_{i}
for the dimension of
H^{2i}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}})
. In our case, we obtain
d_{0}={3\choose 0}=1
,
d_{1}={3\choose 1}=3
, and
d_{2}={3\choose 2}+{3\choose 1}=4
. Alternatively, these numbers can be obtained from a geometric description of the Springer fiber
\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}}
as follows.

Realize
G^{\vee}
as
SO(V)
where
V=
Span
(v_{1},...,v_{7})
, and the symmetric bilinear form is given by
\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle=(-1)^{i+1}\delta_{i,8-j}
. The action of
e^{\vee}
on
V
is given by
e^{\vee}(v_{4})=0
and
e^{\vee}(v_{i})=v_{i-1}
if
i\neq 4
. An element of
\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}}
is a
e^{\vee}
-stable flag
V^{1}\subset V^{2}\subset V^{3}
of isotropic subspaces of
V
. We will use a sequence of vectors
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
to represent the flag with
V^{i}=
Span
(v_{1},...,v_{i})
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant 3
. The Springer fiber
\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}}
has four irreducible components
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}
and
X_{4}
, each
X_{i}
is isomorphic to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. In terms of vectors, they can be described as follows.

The component
X_{1}
consists of the flags of types
V^{2}=
Span
(v_{1},v_{5})
and
v_{3}
is an isotropic vector of Span
(v_{2},v_{4},v_{6})
. The component
X_{2}
consists of the flags of types
(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3})
such that
u_{2}
has the form
av_{1}+bv_{5}+u
with
u\neq 0
isotropic in Span
(v_{2},v_{4},v_{6})
, and
u_{1}=eu_{2}
, while
u_{3}\in
Span
(v_{1},v_{5})
(we require
u_{3}\neq u_{1}
). The component
X_{3}
consists of the flags of types
(v_{1},av_{2}+bv_{5},x(av_{3}+bv_{6})+yv_{5})
, and the component
X_{4}
consists of the flags of types
(v_{5},bv_{6}+av_{1},x(av_{2}+bv_{7})+yv_{1})
. The component group
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
preserves
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, while permutes
X_{3}
and
X_{4}
. For the intersection,
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are disjoint,
X_{3}
and
X_{4}
are disjoint, and the intersections
X_{1}\cap X_{3},X_{1}\cap X_{4},X_{2}\cap X_{3},X_{2}\cap X_{4}
are all isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Comparison of the two algebras
. The socle of
\mathbb{C}[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}]/(x_{1}^{2},x_{2}^{2},x_{3}^{2})
is one-dimensional, generated by the element
x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}
. The socle of
H^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}})
contains
H^{4}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}})
, which has dimension
4
. Thus, the two algebras are not isomorphic; the second statement in
Proposition

6.5
does not hold.

Example 6.7
.

(Failure of flatness condition (

♠

‣
Weak flatness condition
)).
Let
G=Sp(6)
and
G^{\vee}=SO(7)
, they have the same Weyl group
W=S_{3}\rtimes(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus 3}
. Let
e^{\vee}\in G^{\vee}
be a nilpotent element with the associated partition
(3,2,2)
. The refined BVLS duality sends the orbit of
e^{\vee}
in
\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{o}(7)
to the orbit of
e
, a nilpotent element with the associated partition
(3,3)
in
\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{p}(6)
.

In this case,
L^{\vee}
is isomorphic to
SO(3)\times GL(2)
and
L
is isomorphic to
Sp(2)\times GL(2)
. Here
W_{L}\simeq W_{L^{\vee}}\simeq(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus 2}
. Hence, the dimension of the quotient
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]/\gr I
is
|W|/|W_{L}|=\frac{3!\times 2^{3}}{2^{2}}=12
. However, by the table at the end of
[
Kra80
]
, we see that the dimension of
\mathbb{C}[\overline{G.e}\cap\mathfrak{h}]
is
13
. Thus, the flatness conjecture does not hold in this case.

We give the readers a quick explanation for this failure of flatness. Let
e^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{sp}(6)
be a nilpotent element with associated partition
(2,2,2)
, this is the case we have considered in
Example

6.6
. Hence, we know that
I_{e^{\prime}}
is generated by
(x_{i}^{2})_{i=1,2,3}
where
x_{i}
are the coordinate functions of
\mathfrak{h}
. Now, it is clear that
e^{\prime}\in\overline{G.e}
, so
I_{e^{\prime}}\supset I_{e}
. In particular, we deduce that
x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}\notin I_{e}
because
x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}\notin I_{e^{\prime}}
. On the other hand, in the notation of
Example

6.6
, a generic element
x\in\mathfrak{z}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathfrak{l})
has the form diag
(t,t,0,0,-t,-t)
. This means that in the orbit
W.x\subset\mathfrak{h}
, we always have
x_{i}=0
for some
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. In other words,
x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}\in\gr I^{\prime}
, so
\gr I^{\prime}\supsetneq I_{e}
. Statement 1 of
Proposition

6.5
fails here.

Remark 6.8
.

In work in progress, Finkelberg, Hanany, and Nakajima realize this example as Higgs and Coulomb branches
X^{\vee}=\mathcal{M}_{H}
,
X=\mathcal{M}_{C}
of a certain orthosymplectic quiver gauge theory. So, one should not expect in general that if
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{H}
is a resolution of the Higgs branch of some
3
-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=4
supersymmetric gauge theory, then the algebra
H^{*}(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{H})
will be isomorphic to the algebra of schematic fixed point
\mathbb{C}[\mathcal{M}_{C}^{T_{\mathcal{M}_{C}}}]
(see Remark
4.4
above for the statement that might be true).

A straightforward generalization of this phenomenon for flatness condition is the following.

Proposition 6.9
.

Consider
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
.

(1)

If
\mathfrak{l}=\mathfrak{gl}(n)
, then (

♠

‣
Weak flatness condition
) holds.

(2)

If
\mathfrak{l}
contains a nontrivial factor
\mathfrak{sp}_{2m}
, then (

♠

‣
Weak flatness condition
) does not hold.

Proof.

Consider
\mathfrak{l}=\mathfrak{gl}(n)
. A generic element
x\in Z_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathfrak{l})
has the form diag
(t,...,t,-t,...,-t)
for
t\neq 0
. Fix such a
t
, identify
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]
with
\mathbb{C}[x_{1},...,x_{n}]
. The defining ideal
I^{\prime}
of the orbit
W.x\subset\mathfrak{h}
is generated by the elements
(x_{i}^{2}-t^{2})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}
. As a consequence,
\gr I^{\prime}
is
(x_{i}^{2})
. Furthermore, we claim that
I_{e}=\gr I^{\prime}
in this case. Since the partition of
e
is
(2^{n})
, any element
X
in the closure
\overline{G.e}
has the property
X^{2}=0
. So
x_{i}^{2}\in I_{e}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, and
I_{e}=\gr I^{\prime}
. Statement 1 in
Proposition

6.5
is verified. The algebra
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]/I_{e}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}[x_{1},...,x_{n}]/(x_{1}^{2},...,x_{n}^{2})
.

Consider
\mathfrak{l}=\mathfrak{sp}(2a)\times\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathfrak{gl}(b_{i})
for some
a>0
. A generic element
x\in Z_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathfrak{l})
has the following property. In
b_{1}+...+b_{k}+1
arbitrary coordinates of
x
, there is at least one
0
. Hence, we have
x_{1}x_{2}...x_{b_{1}+...+b_{k}+1}\in\gr I^{\prime}
. Then, to prove the proposition, we just need to show that
x_{1}x_{2}...x_{b_{1}+...+b_{k}+1}
does not belong to
I_{e}
.

Write
c_{1}
for
b_{1}+...+b_{k}
. As
a>0
, we have
c_{1}+1\leqslant n
. Consider a matrix realization
\mathfrak{sp}({2n})=\mathfrak{sp}(V)
where
V=\mathbb{C}^{2n}
. We choose a basis
v_{1},...,v_{2n}
of
V
such that the symplectic form is given by
(J_{s},...,J_{s})
where
J_{s}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}
. Write
V^{\prime}
for Span
(v_{1},...,v_{2c_{1}+2})
. We then have an embedding

i_{c_{1}}:\mathfrak{sp}({2c_{1}+2})=\mathfrak{sp}(V^{\prime})\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{sp}(V)=\mathfrak{sp}({2n}).

We write
\mathbb{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}
for the orbit with partition
\mathfrak{p}
. Consider the nilpotent orbit
\mathbb{O}_{(2^{c_{1}+1})}
in
\mathfrak{sp}({2c_{1}+2})
and its image under
i_{c_{1}}
. We see that

i_{c_{1}}(\overline{\mathbb{O}_{(2^{c_{1}+1})}})\subset\overline{\mathbb{O}_{(2^{c_{1}+1},1^{2n-2c_{1}-2})}}\subset\overline{G.e}.

This composition of embedding induces a surjection on the level of scheme theoretic intersection

\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}\cap\overline{G.e}]\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}\cap\overline{\mathbb{O}_{(2^{c_{1}+1})}}].

We see that the element
x_{1}...x_{c_{1}+1}
has a nonzero image in the quotient
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}\cap\overline{\mathbb{O}_{(2^{c_{1}+1})}}]
thanks to the result of part (1). Hence, it is nonzero in
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}\cap\overline{G.e}]
.
∎

Remark 6.10
.

We have discussed the case
\mathfrak{l}\subset\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sp}(2n)
for
\mathfrak{l}
contains a non-trivial factor
\mathfrak{sp}
. If
\mathfrak{l}
is of the form
\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathfrak{gl}({a_{i}})
, it was claimed in
[
Tan82
, Theorem 3]
that Condition 1 in
Proposition

6.5
always holds. The statement of this theorem is correct, but the proof in
[
Tan82
]
contains a mistake (
[
Tan82
, Lemma 7]
is not correct, e.g., for
\mathfrak{l}=\mathfrak{gl}(3)\times\mathfrak{gl}(1)\subset\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{p}(8)
). The correct proof is given in
[
H24a
, Sections 2,3]
.

In the two examples above, we have considered
G^{\vee}
of type
B
and
G
of type
C
. The next example demonstrates this phenomenon for
G^{\vee}
of type
C
and
G
of type
B
. In particular, Condition 2 in
Proposition

6.5
fails (Condition 1 is true, but we do not give proof here). The method to show that the two algebras are not isomorphic is similar to
Example

6.6
.

Example 6.11
.

Let
G=SO(9)
and
G^{\vee}=Sp(8)
. Let
e^{\vee}\in G^{\vee}
be a nilpotent element with the associated partition
(4,2,2)
. The refined BVLS duality sends the orbit of
e^{\vee}
in
\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{p}(8)
to the orbit of
e
, a nilpotent element with the associated partition
(3,3,1,1,1)
in
\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{o}(9)
.

The function side
. In this case, we have
L^{\vee}\simeq Sp(4)\times GL(2)
and
L\simeq SO(5)\times GL(2)
. We choose an embedding
\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{o}(9)\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}(9)
such that we can realize
\mathfrak{h}
inside
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
as the set of diagonal matrices diag
(x_{1},...,x_{4},0,-x_{4},...,-x_{1})
.

A generic element
x\in Z_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathfrak{l})
has the form diag
(t,t,0,0,0,0,0,-t,-t)
for
t\neq 0
. Fix such a
t
, identify
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]
with
\mathbb{C}[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}]
. The orbit
W.x
in
\mathfrak{h}
consists of
24
points
(a,b,c,d)
such that two of the coordinates are 0, and the other two are
t
or
-t
. It is straightforward to see that the following elements belong to
I^{\prime}

\{(x_{i}^{2}-t^{2})x_{i}\}_{i=1,2,3,4};2t^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{4}x_{i}^{2};\{x_{ijk}\}_{1\leqslant i<j<k\leqslant 4};

Let
I^{\prime\prime}
be the ideal generated by these polynomials. Next, one can check that the dimension of
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]/I^{\prime\prime}
(equals to the dimension of
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]/\gr I^{\prime\prime}
) is
24=|W/W_{L}|
. A basis of
\mathbb{C}[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}]/\gr I^{\prime\prime}
is given by
1,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{1}^{2},x_{2}^{2},x_{3}^{2},

\{x_{i}x_{j}\}_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 4},

x_{1}x_{2}^{2},x_{1}x_{3}^{2},x_{2}x_{3}^{2},x_{2}x_{4}^{2},

x_{3}x_{4}^{2},x_{3}x_{1}^{2},

x_{4}x_{1}^{2},x_{4}x_{2}^{2},

x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2},x_{2}^{2}x_{3}^{2}
. Therefore,
I^{\prime}=I^{\prime\prime}
and the socle of
\mathbb{C}[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}]/\gr I^{\prime}
has dimension
2
.

The cohomological side
. The dimension of the Springer fiber
\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}}
is
3
, so the grading does not match the function side since we have elements of degree
4
in
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}]/I_{e}
. Let
d_{i}
be the dimension of
H^{2i}(\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}})
. By the recursive formula in
[
Kim18
, Theorem 6.1]
, we see that the Betti numbers of
\mathcal{B}_{e^{\vee}}
are
d_{0}=1,d_{1}=4,d_{2}=8,d_{3}=11
. The socle of this algebra has dimension at least
11
. Hence, the two algebras are not isomorphic.

7.
Localizations, categories
\mathcal{O}
and Cartan subquotients: case of parabolic
W
-algebras

This section is rather technical and will be used in the proof of the main Theorem in Section
9
.
In this section, we describe the homomorphism (
2.7.3
) explicitly for
Y=\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p})
(see Lemma
7.13
). Additionally, we will discuss the abelian localization theorem for parabolic
W
-algebras.

7.1.
Localization theorem for parabolic
W
-algebras

In this section we describe certain
\lambda\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})
for which the abelian localization holds for
(p(\lambda),\widetilde{S}(\chi,\mathfrak{p}))
(recall that
p\colon H^{2}(T^{*}\mathcal{P},\mathbb{C})\rightarrow H^{2}(\widetilde{S}(\chi,\mathfrak{p}),\mathbb{C})
is the restriction map). This is well-known to experts, but we include the proof for completeness (note that we are not assuming that
e
is regular in Levi in this section).

We start with a description of (some) parameters for which the abelian localization holds for
(\lambda,T^{*}\mathcal{P})
. We fix a Borel subalgebra
T\subset B\subset P
.
To
\lambda\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})
we associate
\widetilde{\lambda}:=\lambda-\rho_{\mathfrak{b}}(\mathfrak{l})
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Abelian localization holds for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})=H^{2}(T^{*}\mathcal{P},\mathbb{C})
if
\widetilde{\lambda}
is

\mathfrak{p}
-antidominant and
\mathfrak{l}
-regular.

Proof.

The claim is proved completely analogically to
[
BB81
, Théor
\grave{\operatorname{e}}
me principal]
(c.f.
[
BK15
, Theorem 5.1]
).
∎

Warning 7.2
.

We are
not
claiming that the parameters as in Proposition
7.1
are the only parameters for which the abelian localization holds.
It also might be true that every
\mathfrak{l}
-regular
\widetilde{\lambda}
is automatically
\mathfrak{p}
-antidominant (this is certainly the case for
\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{b}
).

Remark 7.3
.

Note that if we fix another
T\subset B^{\prime}\subset P
, then the corresponding element
\widetilde{\lambda}^{\prime}:=\lambda-\rho_{\mathfrak{b}^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{l})
will be
\mathfrak{p}
-antidominant
\mathfrak{l}
-regular iff
\widetilde{\lambda}
is
\mathfrak{p}
-antidominant
\mathfrak{l}
-regular. Indeed,
B^{\prime}=wBw^{-1}
for some
w\in W_{L}
so
\widetilde{\lambda}^{\prime}=w(\widetilde{\lambda})
. Now the claim follows from the fact that
w(\Delta_{\mathfrak{p}})=\Delta_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Remark 7.4
.

Note that the condition that
\widetilde{\lambda}=\lambda-\rho_{\mathfrak{b}}(\mathfrak{l})
is
\mathfrak{p}
-antidominant is equivalent to the condition that
\widetilde{\lambda}
is
\mathfrak{b}
-antidominant (use that
\langle\lambda,\beta^{\vee}\rangle=0
for
\beta^{\vee}\in\Delta_{\mathfrak{l}}^{\vee}
). Similarly,
\widetilde{\lambda}
is
\mathfrak{l}
-regular iff it is regular.

Proposition 7.5
.

Pick
\lambda\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})
. Let
p\colon H^{2}(T^{*}\mathcal{P},\mathbb{C})\rightarrow H^{2}(\widetilde{S}(\chi,\mathfrak{p}),\mathbb{C})
be the restriction map. If the abelian localization holds for
(\lambda,T^{*}\mathcal{P})
then the abelian localization holds for
(p(\lambda),\widetilde{S}(e,\mathfrak{p}))
.

Proof.

We use the same approach as in
[
Los19a
, Propositions 3.7, 3.8]
, see also
[
Gin09
]
and
[
DK09
]
. Recall (see Proposition
2.37
(b))
that
\Gamma(\widetilde{S}(\chi,\mathfrak{p}),\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(\widetilde{S}(e,\mathfrak{p})))
identifies with the parabolic
W
-algebra
\mathcal{W}_{\lambda}(\chi,\mathfrak{p})
.

Assume that the abelian localization holds for
(\lambda,T^{*}\mathcal{P})
.
Let us denote by
\operatorname{Coh}^{\mathfrak{u}_{\ell},\chi}(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(T^{*}\mathcal{P}))
the category of twisted
(\mathfrak{u}_{\ell},\chi)
-equivariant coherent
\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(T^{*}\mathcal{P})
-modules and by
\mathcal{U}_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{p})
-
\operatorname{mod}^{\mathfrak{u}_{\ell},\chi}
the category of twisted
(\mathfrak{u}_{\ell},\chi)
-equivariant finitely generated
\mathcal{U}_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{p})
-modules, see
[
Kas89
, Section 4]
for the definitions.

We have the following equivalences (the second one is the parabolic version of the Skryabin equivalence):

(7.1.1)

\operatorname{Coh}^{\mathfrak{u}_{\ell},\chi}(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(T^{*}\mathcal{P}))\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(\widetilde{S}(e,\mathfrak{p}))),\,U_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{p})\text{-}\operatorname{mod}^{\mathfrak{u}_{\ell},\chi}\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,\mathcal{W}_{\lambda}(\chi,\mathfrak{p})\text{-}\operatorname{mod}

given by taking
\mathfrak{u}_{\ell}
-semiinvariants with character
\chi
. Both of these isomorphisms are consequences of the fact that the action of
U_{\ell}
on the affine variety
\mu_{T^{*}\mathcal{P}}^{-1}(\chi)
is free. For the first isomorphism, see
[
DK09
, Lemma 5.3]
or
[
KR08
, Proposition 2.8]
. The second isomorphism can be proved analogously to
[
GG02
, Section 6.2]
, see also
[
Soe86
]
.

It is clear that for
M\in\operatorname{Coh}^{\mathfrak{u}_{\ell},\chi}(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(T^{*}\mathcal{P}))
, we have

(7.1.2)

\Gamma(M)^{(\mathfrak{u}_{\ell},\chi)}=\Gamma(M^{(\mathfrak{u}_{\ell},\chi)}).

We note that the global section functor
\Gamma:\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(T^{*}\mathcal{P}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{U}_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{p})
and localization functor
\operatorname{Loc}:\mathcal{U}_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{p})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(T^{*}\mathcal{P}))
naturally lift to the functors between categories of respective twisted equivariant modules.

Since the abelian localization holds for
(\lambda,T^{*}\mathcal{P})
, these functors are quasi-inverse on the categories of non-equivariant modules. One can see that the twisted equivariant module structure on
\mathcal{M}\in\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(T^{*}\mathcal{P}))
coincides with the induced structure on
\Loc(\Gamma(\mathcal{M}))
, and therefore the global section functor induces an equivalence

\Gamma\colon\operatorname{Coh}^{\mathfrak{u}_{\ell},\chi}(\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(T^{*}\mathcal{P}))\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{p})\text{-}\operatorname{mod}^{\mathfrak{u}_{\ell},\chi}.

It now follows from (
7.1.1
), (
7.1.2
) that the localization holds for
(p(\lambda),\widetilde{S}(\chi,\mathfrak{p}))
.
∎

7.2.
Cartan subquotients and highest weights for finite
W
-algebras

The results of this section follow from
[
BGK08
]
, the only difference is that the authors of loc. cit. work with filtered algebras although we work with algebras over
\mathbb{C}[\hbar]
. Another reference is
[
Los12a
]
.

Recall that
M\subset Q
is the standard Levi, and let
e=e_{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathfrak{m}
be the regular nilpotent that lies in
\mathfrak{b}
. Recall that
\chi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
the linear functional
(e,-)
. Let
Z_{M}\subset M
be the connected component of the center of
M
and
\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{m})=\operatorname{Lie}Z_{M}\subset\mathfrak{m}
. Pick a generic cocharacter
\nu\colon\mathbb{C}^{\times}\rightarrow Z_{M}
such that
\nu
is dominant w.r.t.
Q
(i.e.,
\langle\alpha,\nu\rangle\geqslant 0
for every
\alpha\in\Delta_{\mathfrak{q}}
). Let
\widetilde{\nu}\colon\mathbb{C}^{\times}\rightarrow T
be a generic
B
-dominant cocharacter.

7.2.1.
Definition of
\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi)
via non-linear Lie algebras

Results of this section can be found in
[
BGK08
, Section 2.2]
.

Recall that
(e,h,f)
form an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triple.
Consider the grading
\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{g}(i)
induced by the adjoint action of
h
and set
\mathfrak{r}:=\bigoplus_{i\geqslant 0}\mathfrak{g}(i)
, this is a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
.

Set
\mathfrak{k}=\mathfrak{g}(-1)
and let
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathrm{ne}}
be a
non-linear Lie algebra
(c.f.
[
BGK08
, beginning of Section 2.2]
) with the non-linear Lie bracket given by
[x^{\mathrm{ne}},y^{\mathrm{ne}}]=\langle\chi,e_{\mathfrak{m}},[x,y]\rangle
, where we denote by
x^{\mathrm{ne}},y^{\mathrm{ne}}\in\mathfrak{k}
the elements corresponding to
x,y\in\mathfrak{k}
.
Consider the direct sums
\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}=\mathfrak{r}\oplus\mathfrak{k}^{\mathrm{ne}}
,
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{k}^{\mathrm{ne}}
equipped with the natural structures of the non-linear Lie algebra.

For a vector space
V
, let
T_{\hbar}(V):=(\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}V^{\otimes k})\otimes\mathbb{C}[\hbar]
be the corresponding tensor algebra over
\mathbb{C}[\hbar]
.
Set

\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}):=T_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}})/M_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}),~\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}):=T_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}})/M_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}),

where
M_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}})
is the two-sided ideal in
T_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}})
generated by
a\otimes b-b\otimes a-\hbar[a,b]
,
a,b\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}
and similarly for
M_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}})
.

We define the
Kazhdan grading
on
\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}})
,
\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}})
as follows:

\operatorname{deg}\mathfrak{g}(i)=i+2,~\operatorname{deg}\mathfrak{k}^{\mathrm{ne}}=1,~\operatorname{deg}\hbar=2
.

Let
J_{\hbar}\subset\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}})
be the left ideal generated by the elements
\{x-\chi(x)-x^{\mathrm{ne}},\,|\,x\in\mathfrak{n}\}
(note that this ideal is
homogeneous
w.r.t. the Kazhdan grading). The PBW theorem for
\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}})
gives the projection
\operatorname{Pr}_{\hbar}\colon\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}})\twoheadrightarrow J_{\hbar}
. Let
I_{\hbar}\subset\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
be the left ideal generated by
\{x-\chi(x)\,|\,x\in\mathfrak{u}\}
.

Set

\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\chi)\subset\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}})=\{u\in\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}),\,|\,\operatorname{Pr}([x-x^{\mathrm{ne}},u])=0,~\text{for all}~x\in\mathfrak{n}\}.

Set
Q_{\hbar}=\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})/I_{\hbar}
and recall that
\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi)=Q_{\hbar}^{\mathfrak{n}}
, where
\mathfrak{n}=\bigoplus_{i\leqslant-1}\mathfrak{g}(i)
. The action of
\mathfrak{g}
on
Q_{\hbar}
extends to the action of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}
via the formula
x^{\mathrm{ne}}(u+I_{\hbar})=ux+I_{\hbar}
.
[
BGK08
, Theorem 2.4]
implies the following.

Proposition 7.6
.

Subspace
\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\chi)\subset\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}})
is a subalgebra and the map

\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\chi)\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi),~x\mapsto x(1+I_{\hbar})

is the isomorphism of algebras.

7.2.2.
Explicit description of the Cartan subquotient of
\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi)

In
[
BGK08
]
authors described the Cartan subquotient
\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}(\chi))
. Let us formulate the homogeneous version of their result. For simplicity, we assume that
e=e_{\mathfrak{m}}
is regular in
\mathfrak{m}
as this is the only case we care about in the paper.
Let
\gamma\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
be as in
[
BGK08
, Section 4.1]
it follows from
[
BGK08
, Lemma 4.1]
that
\gamma
extends (uniquely) to the character of
\mathfrak{r}_{0}
. Let
S_{-\gamma}\colon\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{r}_{0})\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{r}_{0})
be the automorphism given by
x\mapsto x-\hbar^{\frac{\operatorname{deg}x}{2}}\gamma(x)
,
x\in\mathfrak{r}
(note that the Kazhdan grading becomes
even
being restricted to
\mathfrak{r}_{0}\subset\mathfrak{m}
).

Cocharacter
\nu\colon\mathbb{C}^{\times}\rightarrow Z_{M}
defines the grading on
\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}})
and on its subalgebra
\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\chi)
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\chi)_{0}\subset\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\chi)_{0}
be the degree zero components. Note that
\mathfrak{g}_{0}=\mathfrak{m}
so
\mathfrak{r}_{0}=\mathfrak{r}\cap\mathfrak{m}
. Moreover, note that
\mathfrak{k}\cap\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}(-1)=0
since the
h
-grading on
\mathfrak{m}
is
even
. It follows that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}=\mathfrak{r}_{0}
. The following lemma is a standard corollary.

Lemma 7.7
.

The natural embedding
\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{r}_{0})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}})_{0}
induces the identification:

(7.2.1)

\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{r}_{0})\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}})).

Let
\pi\colon\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}})_{0}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{r}_{0})
be the composition of the surjection
\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}})_{0}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}))
and the inverse to the isomorphism (
7.2.1
).

Recall that
Z_{\hbar}\subset\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
is the center.
The natural embedding
Z_{\hbar}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi)
induces the embedding
Z_{\hbar}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi))
. We want to understand the algebra
\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(U_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\chi))
. For that, we need the following standard lemma. Let
M
,
N
be nonnegatively graded modules over the graded ring
R=\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}]
,
\operatorname{deg}x_{i}=2
. Assume that
\operatorname{dim}M_{i}<\infty
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
. We also assume that
N
is flat (
=
graded free) over
R
.

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
\varphi\colon M\rightarrow N
be a graded homomorphism. Then
\varphi
is an isomorphism iff its specialization to
0\in\operatorname{Spec}R
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Let us first of all show that the morphism
\varphi
is surjective. Set
\mathfrak{m}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})
. We have an exact sequence
M\rightarrow N\rightarrow\operatorname{coker}\varphi
. Tensoring it by
R/\mathfrak{m}
we obtain an exact sequence
M/\mathfrak{m}M\rightarrow N/\mathfrak{m}N\rightarrow\operatorname{coker}\varphi/\mathfrak{m}\operatorname{coker}\varphi
. The first map in this sequence is an isomorphism that implies
\operatorname{coker}\varphi=\mathfrak{m}\operatorname{coker}\varphi
. Note now that
\operatorname{coker}\varphi
is nonnegative graded and the generators of
\mathfrak{m}
have positive degrees. This implies that
\operatorname{coker}\varphi=0
.

Now, consider the exact sequence
0\rightarrow\operatorname{ker}\varphi\rightarrow M\rightarrow N\rightarrow 0
. Module
N
is flat so after tensoring by
R/\mathfrak{m}
we obtain the exact sequence
0\rightarrow\operatorname{ker}\varphi/\mathfrak{m}\operatorname{ker}\varphi\rightarrow M/\mathfrak{m}M\rightarrow N/\mathfrak{m}N\rightarrow 0
implying that
\operatorname{ker}\varphi/\mathfrak{m}\operatorname{ker}\varphi=0
so
\operatorname{ker}\varphi=0
.
∎

Proposition 7.9
.

The map
S_{-\gamma}\circ\pi\colon\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}})_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{r}_{0})\subset\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{m})
induces the surjective map
\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\chi)_{0}\twoheadrightarrow Z(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{m}))
that, in its turn, induces the isomorphism
\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\chi))\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,Z(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{m}))
.

Proof.

The homogeneous version of the argument used in the proof of
[
BGK08
, Theorem 4.3]
shows that the map
S_{-\gamma}\circ\pi
induces the homomorphism
\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\chi))\rightarrow Z(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{m}))
of graded
\mathbb{C}[\hbar]
-algebras.
Clearly,
Z(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{m}))=\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]^{W_{M}}
is flat over
Z_{\hbar}=\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]^{W}
.
So, by Lemma
7.8
, in order to check that
\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\chi))\rightarrow Z(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{m}))
is an isomorphism it is enough to show that it becomes an isomorphism after the specialization
\hbar=0
.

Let us identify:

\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\chi)\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi)~\text{(see Proposition \ref{alter_def_W})},~\widetilde{HC}_{\hbar}\colon Z(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{m}))\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]^{W_{M}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[\hbar].

It remains to check that the
\hbar=0
specialization of the morphism

(7.2.2)

\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi))\simeq\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\chi))\rightarrow Z(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{m}))\simeq\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]^{W_{M}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[\hbar]

is equal to the isomorphism
\mathbb{C}[S(\chi)^{Z_{M}}]\simeq\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]^{W_{M}}
induced by the natural identification
S(\chi)^{Z_{M}}=S(\chi,\mathfrak{m})\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,\mathfrak{m}^{*}/\!/M
.

Recall that the morphism (
7.2.2
) is induced by the collection of maps:

(7.2.3)

\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi)_{0}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\chi)_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}})_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{m})\supset Z(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{m})).

Recall also that

\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi)/(\hbar)=\mathbb{C}[(\chi+(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{u})^{*})/\!/N],~\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\chi)/(\hbar)=\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}^{*}/\!/N],

\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}})/(\hbar)=\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}^{*}],~\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{m})/(\hbar)=\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{m}^{*}],~Z(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{m}))/(\hbar)=\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{m}^{*}/\!/M].

We will also use the identification
S(\chi)\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,(\chi+(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{u})^{*})/\!/N
induced by the embedding
\chi+\mathfrak{z}_{\mathfrak{g}}(f)\subset\chi+(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{u})^{*}
(we identify
\mathfrak{z}_{\mathfrak{g}}(f)
with its image in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
).

After specializing to
\hbar=0
the maps in (
7.2.3
) become:

\mathbb{C}[\chi+\mathfrak{z}_{\mathfrak{g}}(f)]_{0}\leftarrow\mathbb{C}[(\chi+(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{u})^{*})/\!/N]\leftarrow\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}^{*}/\!/N]_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}^{*}]_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{m}^{*}]\supset\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{m}^{*}]^{M},

where the map
\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}^{*}]_{0}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{m}^{*}]
is induced by the projection
\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}=\mathfrak{r}_{0}
composed with the embedding
\mathfrak{r}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{m}
and all other morphisms are obvious.
It follows that if
s\in\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{\mathfrak{r}}^{*}]
is invariant w.r.t. both
N
and
Z_{M}
, then the induced surjection
\mathbb{C}[\chi+\mathfrak{z}_{\mathfrak{g}}(f)]\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{m}^{*}]^{M}
sends
s|_{\chi+\mathfrak{z}_{\mathfrak{g}}(f)}
to
s|_{\mathfrak{m}^{*}}
. Being restricted to
\chi|_{\mathfrak{m}}+\mathfrak{z}_{\mathfrak{m}}(f)
, it then sends
s|_{(\chi|_{\mathfrak{m}}+\mathfrak{z}_{\mathfrak{m}}(f))}
to
s|_{\mathfrak{m}^{*}}
. Since
s|_{(\chi|_{\mathfrak{m}}+\mathfrak{z}_{\mathfrak{m}}(f))}
is the restriction of
s|_{\mathfrak{m}^{*}}
to
\chi|_{\mathfrak{m}}+\mathfrak{z}_{\mathfrak{m}}(f)
, we are done.
∎

Combining Proposition
7.9
with the identification
\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g},\chi)\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi)
described in Proposition
7.6
together with the identification
\widetilde{HC}_{\hbar}\colon Z(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{m}))\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]^{W_{M}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[\hbar]
, we obtain the isomorphism:

(7.2.4)

\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi))\simeq\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]^{W_{M}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[\hbar].

Example
.

Let’s consider the example
\chi=0
. Then,
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{h}
,
\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(0)=\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
but the identification
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[\hbar]\simeq\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g}))
is
not
just induced by the natural embedding
\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{h})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{\hbar}(\mathfrak{g})
. It is induced by this embedding composed with the twist
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]
given by a pull back w.r.t. the morphism
(\lambda,\hbar_{0})\mapsto(\lambda-\hbar_{0}\rho_{\mathfrak{g}},\hbar_{0})
. We need this shift because we want the center
Z_{\hbar}
to identify with
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]^{W_{G}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[\hbar]
after the identifications above.

The following lemma follows from the proof of Proposition
7.9
above.

Lemma 7.10
.

For
\hbar=0
, the isomorphism (
7.2.4
):
\mathbb{C}[S(\chi)^{Z_{M}}]\simeq\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]^{W_{M}}
is induced by the natural isomorphism
S(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}})^{Z_{M}}=S(\chi,\mathfrak{m})\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,\mathfrak{m}^{*}/\!/M
.

7.2.3.
Highest weights for
\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi)

In this section, we describe the homomorphism (
2.7.3
) for
Y=\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{b})
.

Proposition 7.11
.

The induced embedding

\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]^{W_{G}}\xrightarrow{(HC_{\hbar})^{-1}}Z_{\hbar}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi))\simeq\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi,\mathfrak{m})\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]^{W_{M}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[\hbar]

is given by
s\mapsto\widetilde{s}
, where
\widetilde{s}(\lambda,\hbar_{0})=s(\lambda-\hbar_{0}\rho_{\mathfrak{g}},\hbar_{0})
for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
,
\hbar_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
. In particular, the image of this embedding is equal to
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]^{W_{G}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[\hbar]
.

Proof.

Follows from
[
BGK08
, Theorem 4.7 and Lemma 5.1]
: from their results, it follows that the composition
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]^{W_{G}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[\hbar]\xrightarrow{(\widetilde{HC}_{\hbar})^{-1}}Z_{\hbar}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}(\chi))\simeq\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi,\mathfrak{m})\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]^{W_{M}}
sends
s
to
s
,
it remains to note that the composition
\widetilde{HC}_{\hbar}\circ(HC_{\hbar})^{-1}
is given by
s\mapsto\widetilde{s}
.
∎

We are now ready to describe the composition (
2.7.3
) in the case of finite
W
-algebras.
Recall from the discussion in
Section

2.5.2
the identification
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}}))\simeq\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}})\otimes_{Z_{\hbar}}\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]
.
Under this identification the
\rho_{\mathfrak{g}}
-twisted action of
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]
on
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}}))
corresponds to the
standard
action of
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]
on the tensor product above.

Proposition 7.12
.

After the identification (
7.2.4
) combined with the bijection

\widetilde{S}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}})^{Z_{M}}=\mathcal{B}_{\chi_{\mathfrak{m}}}^{Z_{M}}={}^{M}W

from
Corollary

3.13
, the homomorphism

(7.2.5)

\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]^{W_{M}}\otimes_{Z_{\hbar}}\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]=\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}})))\rightarrow\Gamma(\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}}),\mathcal{D}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}})))=\bigoplus_{w\in{}^{M}W}\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]

is given by
f\otimes g\mapsto(f(w\lambda,\hbar)g)_{w\in{}^{M}W}
.

Proof.

Let us first of all note that by Remark
2.43
and Proposition
7.11
together with
Proposition

2.47
(a), for fixed
\hbar=\hbar_{0}
and a Zariski generic
\lambda\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, morphism (
7.2.5
) is an isomorphism and should send
s\otimes g
to the collection of functions
s(w\lambda,\hbar_{0})g(\lambda,\hbar_{0})
parametrized by
w\in W_{M}\backslash W
. From the continuity (see also Proposition
2.47
(c)), we conclude that this holds for every
\lambda
, so it remains to identify this labeling with the labeling by
\widetilde{S}(e_{\mathfrak{m}})^{Z_{M}}
.
This can be done after the specialization at
\hbar=0
. So, using
Lemma

7.10
, we see that it remains to describe the pull back homomorphism

\mathbb{C}[S(e_{\mathfrak{m}})^{Z_{M}}\times_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}/W_{G}}\mathfrak{h}^{*}]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(e_{\mathfrak{m}})^{Z_{M}}].

The fact that it is indeed given by
s\otimes g\mapsto(s(w\lambda)g)_{w}
follows from Proposition
E.7
: recall that fixed points are pairs
(m_{w\lambda}w\cdot\mathfrak{p},x_{w\lambda})
and the value of
s\in\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*}]^{W_{M}}
at the corresponding fixed point is equal to
s(x_{w\lambda})=s(w\lambda)
.
∎

7.3.
Highest weights for finite parabolic
W
-algebras

Recall from
Section

2.5.2
the isomorphism
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}}))\simeq\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}})\otimes_{Z_{\hbar}}\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]
and the homomorphism
\Phi_{\mathfrak{p},\chi_{\mathfrak{m}}}\colon\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}))
It induces the homomorphism:

\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]^{W_{M}}\otimes_{Z_{\hbar}}\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]\simeq\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{W}_{\hbar}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}}))\otimes_{Z_{\hbar}}\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]\simeq\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{h}^{*}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}})))\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p})))

that we call the
Cartan comoment map
.

The goal of this section is to describe the composition:

(7.3.1)

\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]^{W_{M}}\otimes_{Z_{\hbar}}\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}^{*},\hbar]\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p})))\rightarrow\\
\rightarrow\Gamma(\widetilde{S}_{\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}),\mathcal{D}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p})))=\bigoplus_{w\in{}^{M}(W/W_{L})}\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l}),\hbar].

Lemma 7.13
.

The homomorphism (
7.3.1
) is given by:

s\otimes g\mapsto((\lambda,\hbar_{0})\mapsto s(w\lambda,\hbar_{0})g(\lambda+\hbar_{0}\rho_{{\mathfrak{l}}},\hbar_{0}))_{w\in{}^{M}(W/W_{L})},

where
(\lambda,\hbar_{0})\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})\oplus\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

For
\hbar=1
, the abelian localization holds for integral
\lambda
such that
\widetilde{\lambda}
is
\mathfrak{p}
-antidominant regular (see Propositions
7.1
,
7.5
). Moreover, the homomorphism

\Phi_{\mathfrak{p},\chi_{\mathfrak{m}}}\colon\mathcal{A}_{1,\widetilde{\lambda}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}}))\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{1,\lambda}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}))

is surjective for such
\lambda
(see
[
WJ82
, Theorem 3.8]
). It follows that every irreducible
\mathcal{A}_{1,\lambda}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}))
-module is also irreducible over
\mathcal{A}_{1,\widetilde{\lambda}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}}))
.

Consider now the category
\mathcal{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{1,\widetilde{\lambda}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}})))
and recall that the irreducible object corresponding to
w\in{}^{M}W
has the highest weight equal to
w(\widetilde{\lambda})-\rho_{\mathfrak{g}}
. It follows from
[
Web11
, Theorem 8]
that the category
\mathcal{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{1,\widetilde{\lambda}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}})))
is equivalent to the BGG category
\mathcal{O}
for
\mathcal{U}_{\lambda_{\mathfrak{m}}}(\mathfrak{g})
, where
\lambda_{\mathfrak{m}}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is a
\mathfrak{b}
-antidominant weight with stabilizer
W_{M}
. Under this equivalence,

\mathcal{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{1,\lambda}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p})))\subset\mathcal{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{1,\widetilde{\lambda}}(\widetilde{S}(\chi_{\mathfrak{m}})))
identifies with the
subcategory consisting of objects that are locally finite for the action of
\mathfrak{p}
(i.e., we consider the
singular
block of the
parabolic
BGG category
\mathcal{O}
, see for example
[
Hum08
, Section 9]
).
It follows from the definitions that the equivalence above sends a simple object of
\mathcal{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{1,\widetilde{\lambda}}(\widetilde{S}(e_{\mathfrak{m}})))
corresponding to
w\in{}^{M}W
to the simple of the BGG category
\mathcal{O}
for
\mathcal{U}_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{g})
with the highest weight
\widetilde{\nu}=w^{-1}(\lambda)-\rho_{\mathfrak{g}}
(note that the map
w\mapsto w^{-1}
indeed defines the bijection
{}^{M}W\,\vphantom{j^{X^{2}}}\smash{\overset{\sim}{\vphantom{\rule{0.0pt}{1.99997pt}}\smash{\longrightarrow}}}\,W/W_{M}
). It is a standard result (compare with
[
Hum08
, Section 9.2]
and
[
Gru23
, Section 2]
) that a simple of
\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{\nu}}(\mathcal{U}_{\lambda})
labeled by
wW_{M}
is
\mathfrak{p}
-integrable iff
w
is longest in
W_{L}w
. We conclude that the simple objects in
\mathcal{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{1,\lambda}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p})))\subset\mathcal{O}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{1,\lambda}(\widetilde{S}(e_{\mathfrak{m}})))
are labeled by
{}^{M}(W/W_{L})\hookrightarrow{}^{M}W
, where the embedding sends
wW_{L}\in{}^{M}(W/W_{L})
to the longest representative in
wW_{L}
.

We then conclude from Proposition
7.12

that the homomorphism (
7.3.1
) sends
s\otimes g
to a collection of functions
(\lambda,\hbar_{0})\mapsto s(w\lambda,\hbar_{0})g(\lambda+\hbar_{0}\rho_{{\mathfrak{l}}},\hbar_{0})
parametrized by
{}^{M}(W/W_{L})
(shift by
\hbar_{0}\rho_{\mathfrak{l}}
appears because the homomorphism
\Phi_{\mathfrak{p},e_{\mathfrak{m}}}
is
not

\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l}),\hbar]
-linear, see Warning
2.31
). To determine the labeling, we restrict to
\hbar=0
. As in the proof of Proposition
7.12
, the claim then follows from Proposition
E.7
together with Corollary
E.9
.
∎

8.
Equivariant Hikita-Nakajima conjecture: general approach

In this section, we describe the general approach to the Hikita-Nakajima conjecture that we propose (following
[
KS
]
) as well as its relation to a certain statement proposed by Bullimore, Dimofte, Gaiotto, Hilburn, and Kim in
[
BDGHK18
]
. We also briefly discuss a quantum version of this conjecture proposed by Kamnitzer, Proudfoot, and McBreen in
[
KP21
]
and mention how it fits into the approach. Let us emphasize that the approach is highly based on the ideas of Bellamy, Braverman, Kamnitzer, Losev, Nakajima, Tingley, Webster, Weekes, Yacobi, and their co-authors.

8.1.
Large polynomial algebra

Let
X
,
X^{\vee}
be a pair of symplectically dual varieties
X
,
X^{\vee}
, and assume that both
X
and
X^{\vee}
admit symplectic resolutions
Y
and
Y^{\vee}
respectively.

Warning 8.1
.

Recall that in the statement of Hikita-Nakajima conjecture, we only assume that
X^{\vee}
has a symplectic resolution.

Our goal is to compare the
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{t}_{X^{\vee}},\hbar]
-algebras:

(8.1.1)

H^{*}_{T_{X^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(Y^{\vee}),\qquad\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{h}_{X}}(X)).

Let us first note that both algebras should carry an additional structure. Namely, recall (see Section
4.1
) that the symplectic duality predicts identification of vector spaces
\mathcal{A}^{2}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{h}_{X},0}\simeq H^{2}_{T_{X^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(Y^{\vee})
.
Moreover, note that the elements of these vector spaces considered as elements of algebras (
8.1.1
) commute with each other. For
H^{2}_{T_{X^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(Y^{\vee})
the claim is clear, and for
\mathcal{A}^{2}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{h}_{X},0}
it follows from the exact sequence (
4.1.1
). Thus, both algebras are not only
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{t}_{X^{\vee}},\hbar]
-algebras but also modules over the bigger polynomial algebra
S^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}^{2}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{h}_{X},0})=S^{\bullet}(H^{2}_{T_{X^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(Y^{\vee}))
.

In fact, in many cases, there is an even bigger polynomial algebras acting on both of the algebras above. For example, assume that
Y^{\vee}=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{H}
is a Nakajima quiver variety corresponding to some quiver
I
, and let
X=\mathcal{M}_{C}
be the corresponding Coulomb branch, and
A
be a flavor torus. Then
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{a}}=H_{*}^{(G_{I}[[t]]\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times})\times A}(\mathcal{R})
is naturally a module over the polynomial algebra
B:=H^{*}_{G_{I}\times A\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\operatorname{pt})
(the subalgebra
B\cdot 1\subset\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{a}}
is sometimes called the “Cartan” subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{a}}
, see e.g.
[
BFN18
, Definition 3.16]
). The same algebra
B
acts naturally on
H^{*}_{A\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{H})=H^{*}_{G_{I}\times A\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\mu^{-1}(0)^{\mathrm{st}})
via the natural restriction homomorphism (which is surjective by the results of McGerty and Nevins, see
[
MN18
]
):

B=H^{*}_{G_{I}\times A\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\mu^{-1}(0))\twoheadrightarrow H^{*}_{G_{I}\times A\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\mu^{-1}(0)^{\mathrm{st}})=H^{*}_{A\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{H}).

Remark 8.2
.

The appearance of the algebra
B
on both sides of the symplectic duality and its importance for the Hikita-Nakajima conjecture was already observed in
[
HKW23
, Section 5.7]
.

Let us list another example which is relevant to the subject of the paper. Let
Y^{\vee}=\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{p}^{\vee},\mathfrak{q}^{\vee})
and
X=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p})]
. Then
H^{*}_{T_{e^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{p}^{\vee},\mathfrak{q}^{\vee}))
is a module over
H^{*}_{T_{e^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(T^{*}\mathcal{Q}^{\vee})
via the restriction map. The latter algebra is a quotient of the polynomial algebra
B=H^{*}_{T_{e^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(\operatorname{pt})\otimes H^{*}_{L}(\operatorname{pt})
(see
Corollary

3.2
). Now, the morphism
\Psi_{\chi,\mathfrak{p}}\colon\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{b}))\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p}))
constructed in Section
2.5.2
realizes
\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{p})))
as a module over
\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{l})}(\widetilde{S}(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{b})))=H^{*}_{T_{e^{\vee}}}(T^{*}\mathcal{Q}^{\vee})
. So, the algebra
B
indeed acts on both sides of the picture.

Remark 8.3
.

Note that in the Coulomb branch case,
B\cdot 1
is a maximal commutative subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{h}_{X}}
and it defines the integrable system on
\mathcal{M}_{C}
. We do not know if this should be a general phenomenon. In the case of Slodowy varieties, one might wonder if the algebra
B\cdot 1
should correspond to some commutative subalgebra in the
W
-algebra. See also a recent preprint
[
Tu
]
.

8.2.
Main conjecture

So, let
B
be some polynomial algebra that acts naturally on both of our algebras. Acting on
1
, we obtain the homomorphisms:

B\xrightarrow{a}H^{*}_{T_{X^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}(Y^{\vee}),~B\xrightarrow{b}\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{h}_{X}}(X)).

Recall that we have the
natural
identification

H^{*}_{T_{X^{\vee}}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}}((Y^{\vee})^{T_{X^{\vee}}})\simeq\Gamma(Y_{\mathfrak{h}_{X}}^{T_{X}},\mathcal{C}_{\nu}(\mathcal{D}_{\hbar,\mathfrak{h}_{X}}(Y)))).

induced by the bijection
(Y^{\vee})^{T_{X^{\vee}}}\simeq Y^{T_{X}}
together with the isomorphism
\mathfrak{t}_{X^{\vee}}\simeq\mathfrak{h}_{X}
(see Section
4.1
).
Note that both of the algebras above are simply
\bigoplus_{p\in Y^{T_{X}}}\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{h}_{X},\hbar]
.

8.2.1.
Statement of the main conjecture

The following Conjecture should be compared with
[
HKW23
, Conjecture 5.26]
and
[
KTWWY19
, Conjecrture 8.10]
.

Conjecture 8.4
.

The following diagram is commutative:

(8.2.1)
